## GHSA-m4w4-3r8h-jw98 · agno

2026-10-27 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The application used `eval()` on user-controlled input for the `field_type` parameter within `FunctionCall` and `UserInputField` objects. This allowed an attacker to inject and execute arbitrary Python code by providing a malicious string as the `field_type`.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve arbitrary code execution on the server, leading to full compromise of the system, including data exfiltration, modification, or denial of service.

### Key diff

```
-                        python_type = eval(field_type) if isinstance(field_type, str) else field_type
+                        type_mapping = {
+                            "str": str,
+                            "int": int,
+                            "float": float,
+                            "bool": bool,
+                            "list": list,
+                            "dict": dict,
+                        }
+                        python_type = type_mapping.get(field_type, str)
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the use of `eval()` for processing the `field_type`. Instead, it now uses a predefined dictionary (`type_mapping`) to map allowed string representations of types (e.g., 'str', 'int') to their corresponding Python type objects, effectively sanitizing the input and preventing arbitrary code execution.

## GHSA-6hp3-vr39-rqw8 · monai

2026-10-27 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.3 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The application did not validate the numerical values extracted from DICOM metadata tags (e.g., ImageOrientationPatient, ImagePositionPatient, PixelSpacing) for finiteness. If these values were non-finite (NaN or Infinity), they could propagate into the affine matrix calculation, leading to a server crash (due to `np.isfinite().all()` on a non-finite array) or silent corruption of inference results.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious DICOM file with non-finite values in specific tags, causing the MONAILabel server to crash when processing the image. Alternatively, it could lead to silent corruption of medical image inference results, potentially affecting diagnostic accuracy.

### Key diff

```
+        def _raise_if_not_finite(values: Sequence[Any], tag: str) -> None:
+            if not np.isfinite(tuple(values)).all():
+                raise ValueError(
+                    f"PydicomReader: cannot derive affine matrix because DICOM tag {tag} "
+                    f"has a non-finite value: {values}."
+                )
         # "00200037" is the tag of `ImageOrientationPatient`
         rx, ry, rz, cx, cy, cz = metadata["00200037"]["Value"]
+        _raise_if_not_finite((rx, ry, rz, cx, cy, cz), "ImageOrientationPatient (0020,0037)")
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new helper function `_raise_if_not_finite` to explicitly check if DICOM metadata values are finite before they are used in affine matrix calculations. It also adds a final check to ensure the composed affine matrix itself is finite, preventing non-finite values from propagating further.

## GHSA-6v38-4h7w-rj9h · authlib

2026-10-27 · Python · **CRITICAL** 9.1 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the JWS JSON deserialization process did not explicitly check if the 'signatures' array was empty or null. If an attacker provided a JWS JSON object with an empty 'signatures' array, the system would proceed without validating any signatures, effectively bypassing the integrity check.

### Impact

An attacker could forge JWS tokens without needing a valid signature, leading to unauthorized access, data tampering, or other security bypasses depending on how the JWS tokens are used within the application.

### Key diff

```
--- a/authlib/jose/rfc7515/jws.py
+++ b/authlib/jose/rfc7515/jws.py
@@ -204,6 +204,11 @@ def deserialize_json(self, obj, key, decode=None):
 
         headers = []
         is_valid = True
+        if not obj["signatures"]:
+            is_valid = False
         for header_obj in obj["signatures"]:
             jws_header, valid = self._validate_json_jws(
                 payload_segment, payload, header_obj, key
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check at the beginning of the `deserialize_json` method to ensure that the `obj["signatures"]` array is not empty or null. If it is, the `is_valid` flag is immediately set to `False`, preventing further processing and ensuring that JWS objects without signatures are rejected.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 71 occurrences**

## GHSA-965c-763c-88jm · better-auth

2026-10-27 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 9.1 · Pattern: `TYPE_CONFUSION→BYPASS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from a lack of cryptographic separation between different purposes of encrypted data. Specifically, the OAuth state parameter, which could contain sensitive user information or be used to initiate a login flow, was encrypted using the same key as other less sensitive data. This allowed an attacker to craft a malicious OAuth state that, when decrypted and processed, could be interpreted as a Magic Link, leading to an authentication bypass.

### Impact

An attacker could leverage a crafted OAuth state to sign in as another user, effectively bypassing authentication and gaining unauthorized access to their account.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/better-auth/src/plugins/oauth-proxy/index.ts
+++ b/packages/better-auth/src/plugins/oauth-proxy/index.ts
@@ -167,7 +171,9 @@ export const oAuthProxy = <O extends OAuthProxyOptions>(opts?: O) => {
 	const maxAge = opts?.maxAge ?? 60; // Default 60 seconds
 	const getEncryptionKey = (
 		ctx: GenericEndpointContext,
- 	): string | SecretConfig => opts?.secret ?? ctx.context.secretConfig;
+ 		purpose: EncryptionPurpose,
+ 	): string | SecretConfig =>
+ 		derivePurposeKey(opts?.secret ?? ctx.context.secretConfig, purpose);
 
 	const oauthProxyCompletion = createAuthEndpoint(
 		"/callback/:id/oauth-proxy",
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces cryptographic purpose separation by deriving distinct encryption keys for different data types (e.g., 'oauth-proxy-profile', 'oauth-proxy-package', 'oauth-proxy-state', 'oauth-state-cookie'). This ensures that data intended for one purpose cannot be maliciously reinterpreted for another, preventing the OAuth state from being misused as a Magic Link. Additionally, it enforces strict schema validation for Magic Link records.

## GHSA-wf6j-7hvr-gr93 · hydra-core

2026-10-27 · Python · **HIGH** 7.8 · Pattern: `DESERIALIZATION→RCE`

### Root cause

The application's execution policy, designed to prevent uncontrolled code execution, had an incomplete blacklist. It failed to explicitly block dangerous PyYAML functions, specifically those that allow arbitrary Python object loading and constructor dispatch, thereby enabling deserialization of untrusted data.

### Impact

An attacker could supply specially crafted YAML input that, when deserialized, would lead to arbitrary code execution on the system running the application.

### Key diff

```
--- a/hydra/_internal/execution_policy.py
+++ b/hydra/_internal/execution_policy.py
@@ -356,6 +356,9 @@
     # unpickles a counts file. The inert Trace constructor is excepted below.
     "shelve.",
     "trace.",
+    # PyYAML exposes Python-object loading and constructor dispatch. Keep only
+    # exact safe load/dump functions available.
+    "yaml.",
     # pydoc imports/executes modules and files (importfile runs a file,
     # safeimport imports by name). Inert documentation formatters are excepted
     # below.
@@ -378,6 +381,10 @@
         "pydoc.HTMLDoc",
         "pydoc.TextDoc",
         "trace.Trace",
+        "yaml.safe_load",
+        "yaml.safe_load_all",
+        "yaml.safe_dump",
+        "yaml.safe_dump_all",
     }
 )
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds 'yaml.' to the list of uncontrolled execution targets, effectively blocking all PyYAML functions by default. It then explicitly whitelists only the safe PyYAML functions ('yaml.safe_load', 'yaml.safe_load_all', 'yaml.safe_dump', 'yaml.safe_dump_all') to ensure only secure deserialization and serialization operations are permitted.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 33 occurrences**

## GHSA-45c3-73f7-pv4m · dnspython

2026-10-27 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→INFINITE_LOOP`

### Root cause

The `dns.name.from_wire_parser()` function did not limit the number of compression pointer dereferences when parsing a DNS name from wire format. An attacker could craft a DNS message with a long chain of pointers, causing the parser to enter an effectively infinite loop or perform excessive work.

### Impact

An attacker could send a specially crafted DNS message to a system using the dnspython library, leading to high CPU utilization and a denial of service for the application or server processing the malicious input.

### Key diff

```
--- a/dns/name.py
+++ b/dns/name.py
@@ -1149,6 +1156,7 @@ def from_wire_parser(parser: dns.wirebase.Parser) -> Name:
 
     labels = []
     biggest_pointer = parser.current
+    hops = 0
     with parser.restore_furthest():
         count = parser.get_uint8()
         while count != 0:
@@ -1159,6 +1167,9 @@ def from_wire_parser(parser: dns.wirebase.Parser) -> Name:
                 if current >= biggest_pointer:
                     raise BadPointer
                 biggest_pointer = current
+                hops += 1
+                if hops > MAX_COMPRESSION_POINTER_CHAIN:
+                    raise PointerChainTooLong
                 parser.seek(current)
             else:
                 raise BadLabelType
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `MAX_COMPRESSION_POINTER_CHAIN` constant set to 16. It adds a counter (`hops`) to track the number of pointer dereferences. If this count exceeds the maximum, a `PointerChainTooLong` exception is raised, preventing excessive looping.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 33 occurrences**

## GHSA-f897-62hf-6gxq · @modelcontextprotocol/hono

2026-10-27 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The HTTP server transports and handlers in the MCP TypeScript SDK did not impose limits on the size of incoming request bodies or the length of JSON-RPC batches. This allowed an attacker to send arbitrarily large requests, causing the server to consume excessive memory and CPU resources.

### Impact

An attacker could send a large request body or a long JSON-RPC batch, leading to resource exhaustion (memory, CPU) on the server. This could result in a denial of service, making the application unavailable to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/middleware/node/src/toNodeHandler.ts
+++ b/packages/middleware/node/src/toNodeHandler.ts
@@ -237,9 +291,17 @@ export async function toWebRequest(req: NodeIncomingMessageLike, parsedBody?: un
     let body: string | undefined;
     if (method !== 'GET' && method !== 'HEAD') {
         if (parsedBody === undefined) {
+            if (Number(singleHeaderValue(req.headers['content-length'])) > maxRequestBodySize) {
+                throw new RequestBodyTooLargeError(maxRequestBodySize);
+            }
             const decoder = new TextDecoder();
             let collected = '';
+            let received = 0;
             for await (const chunk of req) {
+                received += typeof chunk === 'string' ? new TextEncoder().encode(chunk).byteLength : (chunk as Uint8Array).byteLength;
+                if (received > maxRequestBodySize) {
+                    throw new RequestBodyTooLargeError(maxRequestBodySize);
+                }
                 collected += typeof chunk === 'string' ? chunk : decoder.decode(chunk as Uint8Array, { stream: true });
             }
             collected += decoder.decode();
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `maxRequestBodySize` option to limit the size of request bodies processed by the `toNodeHandler` and `toWebRequest` functions. Requests exceeding this limit are now rejected with a 413 'Payload Too Large' error, preventing resource exhaustion. A new `RequestBodyTooLargeError` class was added for specific error handling.

## GHSA-m65p-9j7q-6w33 · mlflow

2026-10-27 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The MLflow server's `_create_model_version` endpoint, when handling prompt model versions, did not adequately validate the `source` parameter. It attempted to block `file://` URIs and absolute paths but failed to account for schemeless paths (e.g., `../etc/passwd`) that would be interpreted as local filesystem paths by the underlying artifact repository, leading to path traversal.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could craft a malicious `source` parameter to read arbitrary files from the MLflow server's filesystem, potentially exfiltrating sensitive configuration files, credentials, or other data.

### Key diff

```
--- a/mlflow/server/handlers.py
+++ b/mlflow/server/handlers.py
@@ -3134,21 +3163,7 @@ def _create_model_version():
 
     is_prompt = _is_prompt_request(request_message)
     if is_prompt:
-        # Prompt sources must not point to local filesystem paths.
-        # Block file:// URIs and absolute paths (e.g. /etc/passwd) but allow
-        # the legitimate schemeless placeholder sources used internally
-        # (e.g. "prompt-template", "dummy-source").
-        source = request_message.source
-        parsed = urllib.parse.urlparse(source)
-        if parsed.scheme == "file" or (parsed.scheme == "" and source.startswith("/")):
-            raise MlflowException(
-                f"Invalid prompt source: '{source}'. "
-                "Local source paths are not allowed for prompts.",
-                INVALID_PARAMETER_VALUE,
-            )
-        # Only validate traversal for sources with a URL scheme (http, https, etc.)
-        if parsed.scheme:
-            _validate_non_local_source_contains_relative_paths(source)
+        _validate_prompt_source(request_message.source)
     else:
         if request_message.model_id:
             _validate_source_model(request_message.source, request_message.model_id)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new validation function `_validate_prompt_source` that strictly enforces allowed `source` values for prompt model versions. It now explicitly rejects all local filesystem paths (schemeless or `file://`) unless they are specific, known internal placeholders, preventing path traversal and arbitrary file reads.

## GHSA-mgcc-cww5-fp2q · github.com/go-jose/go-jose/v3

2026-10-27 · Go · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the library did not properly validate the size of RSA moduli in JSON Web Keys (JWKs) or JSON Web Encryption (JWE) ephemeral public keys (EPK) before processing them. An attacker could provide a JWK with an extremely large RSA modulus, leading to excessive computation during parsing and cryptographic operations.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious JWK or JWE header containing an RSA key with a very large modulus, causing the server to consume significant CPU resources and memory, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/jwk.go
+++ b/jwk.go
@@ -505,13 +521,25 @@ func (k *JSONWebKey) Valid() bool {
 	return true
 }
 
+// MaxRSAModulusBits is the largest RSA modulus size, in bits, that will be
+// accepted when parsing a JWK. Applications that need larger keys can raise
+// this limit.
+//
+// If modifying MaxRSAModulusBits, do it before any parsing occurs.
+var MaxRSAModulusBits = 4096
+
 func (key rawJSONWebKey) rsaPublicKey() (*rsa.PublicKey, error) {
 	if key.N == nil || key.E == nil {
 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("go-jose/go-jose: invalid RSA key, missing n/e values")
 	}
 
+	n := key.N.bigInt()
+	if n.BitLen() > MaxRSAModulusBits {
+		return nil, fmt.Errorf("go-jose/go-jose: invalid RSA key, modulus too large")
+	}
+
 	return &rsa.PublicKey{
-		N: key.N.bigInt(),
+		N: n,
 		E: key.E.toInt(),
 	}, nil
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a maximum allowed RSA modulus size (MaxRSAModulusBits) and validates this limit during JWK parsing. It also prevents the unmarshaling of private key material when parsing public keys from untrusted sources (e.g., 'epk' and 'jwk' headers) and adds checks for the consistency of RSA private key parameters (p*q=n).

## GHSA-qq8j-7v2v-3rwp · undici

2026-10-27 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The undici WebSocket client failed to properly close HTTP/2 streams when the WebSocket handshake failed due to invalid server responses. This led to a resource leak where streams remained open, eventually exhausting available resources and causing a Denial of Service.

### Impact

An attacker could send malformed WebSocket handshake responses to an undici client, causing it to accumulate open HTTP/2 streams. This resource exhaustion would lead to the client becoming unresponsive and unable to process new requests, effectively denying service to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
-          failWebsocketConnection(handler, 1002, 'Received network error or non-101 status code.', response.error)
+          failHandshake(handler, response, 1002, 'Received network error or non-101 status code.', response.error)
...
+function failHandshake (handler, response, code, reason, cause) {
+  if (response.socket?.session != null && !response.socket.destroyed) {
+    response.socket.destroy()
+  }
+  failWebsocketConnection(handler, code, reason, cause)
}
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `failHandshake` function that, in addition to calling the existing `failWebsocketConnection`, explicitly destroys the underlying socket if it's an HTTP/2 session and not already destroyed. This ensures that HTTP/2 streams are properly closed when the WebSocket handshake fails, preventing resource leaks.

## GHSA-qrxm-fqhp-jwvm · undici

2026-10-27 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The `undici` HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 clients used `assert` statements to prevent `ERR_TLS_CERT_ALTNAME_INVALID` errors from propagating. This assertion would crash the process if a TLS 1.2 renegotiation occurred with a changed certificate, which could be triggered by a malicious server.

### Impact

An attacker could cause the client application using `undici` to crash, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js
+++ b/lib/dispatcher/client-h2.js
@@ -293,8 +293,6 @@ function onHttp2SendPing (session) {
 }
 
 function onHttp2SessionError (err) {
-  assert(err.code !== 'ERR_TLS_CERT_ALTNAME_INVALID')
-
   this[kSocket][kError] = err
   this[kClient][kOnError](err)
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the `assert` statements that specifically checked for `ERR_TLS_CERT_ALTNAME_INVALID` errors in both HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 client error handlers. This allows the error to be handled gracefully instead of crashing the process.

## GHSA-2m5v-pch6-74hx · undici

2026-10-27 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `INSECURE_DEFAULT→CONFIG`

### Root cause

The `undici` Agent constructor was not correctly propagating the `tls` options provided by the user. Specifically, the `tls` object was not destructured from the constructor arguments, causing it to be dropped and not included in the internal options used for establishing connections. This meant that any custom TLS validation settings, such as `rejectUnauthorized: true`, were ignored.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially perform man-in-the-middle attacks against applications using `undici` if they expected custom TLS options (like strict certificate validation) to be enforced. This could lead to interception and manipulation of sensitive data.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/dispatcher/agent.js
+++ b/lib/dispatcher/agent.js
@@ -22,7 +22,7 @@ function defaultFactory (origin, opts) {
 }
 
 class Agent extends DispatcherBase {
-  constructor ({ factory = defaultFactory, maxOrigins = Infinity, connect, ...options } = {}) {
+  constructor ({ factory = defaultFactory, maxOrigins = Infinity, connect, tls, ...options } = {}) {
     if (typeof factory !== 'function') {
       throw new InvalidArgumentError('factory must be a function.')
     }
@@ -41,7 +41,11 @@ class Agent extends DispatcherBase {
       connect = { ...connect }
     }
 
-    this[kOptions] = { ...util.deepClone(options), maxOrigins, connect }
+    if (tls && typeof tls !== 'function') {
+      tls = { ...tls }
+    }
+
+    this[kOptions] = { ...util.deepClone(options), maxOrigins, connect, tls }
     this[kFactory] = factory
     this[kClients] = new Map()
     this[kOrigins] = new Set()
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `Agent` constructor to explicitly destructure the `tls` option from the input arguments. It also ensures that if `tls` is provided and is not a function, it is shallow-cloned before being stored in the internal options, thereby preserving user-defined TLS settings.

## GHSA-4w7w-5xrc-9h26 · undici

2026-10-27 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The `undici` HTTP client did not properly handle cross-origin redirects when caching responses. It failed to verify if the final response's origin matched the original request's origin for the cache key. Additionally, it did not honor restrictive `Cache-Control` headers (like `no-store` or `private`) from intermediate redirect hops, allowing a non-cacheable redirect to be laundered into a cacheable final response.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious redirect chain to poison the cache for a trusted origin, leading to cross-user information disclosure or serving of attacker-controlled content to other users who request the original trusted URL.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/handler/cache-handler.js
+++ b/lib/handler/cache-handler.js
@@ -267,6 +296,14 @@ class CacheHandler {
     // would let a redirect target control an entry for a URL the application
     // trusts. The final request is the last URL in the redirect history; only
     // cache when its origin still matches the cache key origin.
+    if (this.#history && this.#history.length > 0) {
+      const finalUrl = this.#history[this.#history.length - 1]
+      if (!sameOrigin(finalUrl.origin, this.#cacheKey.origin)) {
+        return downstreamOnHeaders()
+      }
+    }
+
     // A redirect hop that is not forwarded can still carry a restrictive
     // storage policy (for example `no-store`, or `private` for a shared cache).
     // Honor it, otherwise a redirect that must not be stored could be laundered
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `sameOrigin` check to ensure that a response is only cached if its final origin (after redirects) matches the cache key's origin. It also stores and evaluates `Cache-Control` headers from all intermediate redirect hops, preventing caching if any hop specifies `no-store` or `private` directives.

## GHSA-7vr2-2fgp-9w7p · authlib

2026-10-27 · Python · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an incomplete fix for algorithm confusion, specifically in how Authlib handled public keys. It allowed an attacker to prefix a public key with HMAC algorithm markers, tricking the system into verifying a JWT signed with a symmetric HMAC key using a public key, effectively forging the signature.

### Impact

An attacker could forge JWTs, bypassing signature validation and potentially gaining unauthorized access or impersonating legitimate users within systems that rely on Authlib for JWT verification.

### Key diff

```
--- a/authlib/jose/rfc7518/oct_key.py
+++ b/authlib/jose/rfc7518/oct_key.py
@@ -77,9 +84,7 @@ def import_key(cls, raw, options=None):
         else:
             raw_key = to_bytes(raw)
 
-            # security check
-            if raw_key.startswith(POSSIBLE_UNSAFE_KEYS):
-                raise ValueError("This key may not be safe to import")
+            cls._check_key(raw_key)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a security check (`_check_key`) during key import to prevent keys from containing markers associated with unsafe or confusing algorithms (like 'BEGIN PUBLIC KEY' or 'BEGIN RSA PUBLIC KEY'). It also enforces mandatory audience validation for JWTs, rejecting assertions if no audience is configured.

## GHSA-8m4g-j4wp-wgcg · Authlib

2026-10-27 · Python · **HIGH** 7.4 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The Authlib library's implementation for handling HMAC keys in JWS/JWT signatures allowed an empty string as a valid key. This bypassed the cryptographic security intended by HMAC, as an empty key effectively makes the signature predictable or forgeable.

### Impact

An attacker could forge JWS/JWT signatures that use HMAC algorithms (HS256, HS384, HS512), potentially leading to unauthorized access, privilege escalation, or data manipulation in systems relying on these signatures for authentication or integrity.

### Key diff

```
--- a/authlib/jose/rfc7518/oct_key.py
+++ b/authlib/jose/rfc7518/oct_key.py
@@ -65,6 +65,10 @@ def validate_raw_key(cls, key):
 
     @staticmethod
     def _check_key(raw_key):
+        # Per RFC 7518 §3.2, an HMAC key must be at least as long as the hash output.
+        if not raw_key:
+            raise ValueError("oct key material must not be empty")
+
         # PEM parsers accept leading text, including comments and whitespace.
         if any(marker in raw_key for marker in POSSIBLE_UNSAFE_KEYS):
             raise ValueError("This key may not be safe to import")
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds a check in the `_check_key` method of `oct_key.py` to ensure that the raw key material for HMAC is not empty. If an empty key is provided, a `ValueError` is raised, preventing its use in cryptographic operations.

## GHSA-2v3c-qxhr-7vpv · symfony/yaml

2026-10-27 · PHP · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→INFINITE_LOOP`

### Root cause

The YAML inline lexer in Symfony did not properly handle certain malformed YAML inputs, specifically related to merge keys and tag evaluation. This could lead to unbounded recursion when parsing a specially crafted YAML document, causing the process to exhaust resources and crash.

### Impact

An attacker could provide a malicious YAML document to an application using the Symfony YAML component, leading to a denial of service by crashing the application process. This would make the application unavailable to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/Symfony/Component/Yaml/Inline.php
+++ b/src/Symfony/Component/Yaml/Inline.php
@@ -524,9 +526,15 @@ private static function parseMapping(ParserState $state, string $mapping, int $f
                     throw new ParseException('Missing mapping key.', self::$parsedLineNumber + 1, $mapping);
                 }
 
-                if ('!php/const' === $key || '!php/enum' === $key) {
+                $isKeyEvaluated = '!php/const' === $key || '!php/enum' === $key;
+
+                if ($isKeyEvaluated) {
                     $key .= ' '.self::parseScalar($mapping, $flags, ['(?<!:):(?!:)'], $i, false);
                     $key = self::evaluateScalar($state, $key, $flags);
+
+                    if (!\is_string($key) && !\is_int($key)) {
+                        throw new ParseException((is_numeric($key) ? 'Numeric' : 'Non-string').' keys are not supported. Quote your evaluable mapping keys instead.', self::$parsedLineNumber + 1, $mapping);
+                    }
                 }
 
                 if (self::$objectForMap && \is_string($key) && str_starts_with($key, "\0")) {
@@ -537,7 +545,7 @@ private static function parseMapping(ParserState $state, string $mapping, int $f
                     break;
                 }
 
-                if (!$isKeyQuoted) {
+                if (!$isKeyQuoted && !$isKeyEvaluated) {
                     $evaluatedKey = self::evaluateScalar($state, $key, $flags, $references);
 
                     if ('' !== $key && $evaluatedKey !== $key && (!\is_string($evaluatedKey) || '!' === $key[0]) && !\is_int($evaluatedKey)) {
@@ -549,7 +557,7 @@ private static function parseMapping(ParserState $state, string $mapping, int $f
                     throw new ParseException('Colons must be followed by a space or an indication character (i.e. " ", ",", "[", "]", "{", "}").', self::$parsedLineNumber + 1, $mapping);
                 }
 
-                $isMergeKey = !$isKeyQuoted && '<<' === $key;
+                $isMergeKey = !$isKeyQuoted && !$isKeyEvaluated && '<<' === $key;
 
                 if ($isMergeKey) {
                     $allowOverwrite = true;
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces stricter validation for merge keys and tag evaluation within the YAML inline lexer. It adds checks to prevent non-string/non-integer keys when evaluating `!php/const` or `!php/enum` tags and ensures that merge keys are not evaluated. It also improves error handling in the `FormRenderer` to ensure the variable stack is always cleared, even if rendering fails.

## GHSA-3633-r77q-h3v3 · ddtrace

2026-10-27 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The regular expression used for obfuscating sensitive information in URL query strings contained patterns that were susceptible to catastrophic backtracking. Specifically, the `(?ix)` flag (which includes `x` for verbose mode, allowing whitespace and comments) was removed, and the JWT token matching pattern was made more specific by anchoring it to a word boundary or start of string, and by making the trailing `(?:=|%3D)*` non-greedy. The original regex could lead to excessive processing time when matching certain crafted input strings.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious URL query string that, when processed by the `dd-trace-py` library for obfuscation, would consume excessive CPU resources, leading to a denial of service for the application or service using the library.

### Key diff

```
--- a/ddtrace/settings/_config.py
+++ b/ddtrace/settings/_config.py
@@ -48,7 +48,7 @@
 ENDPOINT_FETCHED_CONFIG = fetch_config_from_endpoint()
 
 DD_TRACE_OBFUSCATION_QUERY_STRING_REGEXP_DEFAULT = (
-    r"(?ix)"
+    r"(?i)"
     r"(?:"  # JSON-ish leading quote
     r'(?:"|%22)?'
     r")"
@@ -81,7 +81,8 @@
     r'(?:"|%22)'  # closing '"' at end of value
     r")"
     r"|(?:"  # other common secret values
-    r" bearer(?:\s|%20)+[a-z0-9._\-]+"
+    r"bearer(?:\s|%20)+[a-z0-9._\-]+"
     r"|token(?::|%3A)[a-z0-9]{13}"
     r"|gh[opsu]_[0-9a-zA-Z]{36}"
-    r"|ey[I-L](?:[\w=-]|%3D)+\.ey[I-L](?:[\w=-]|%3D)+(?:\.(?:[\w.+/=-]|%3D|%2F|%2B)+)?"
+    r"|(^|[^\w%-]|%[0-9a-f]{2})ey[I-L][\w-]+(?:=|%3D)*\.ey[I-L][\w-]+(?:=|%3D)*"
+    r"(?:\.(?:[\w.+/=-]|%3D|%2F|%2B)+)?"
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the regular expression used for obfuscating sensitive data in URL query strings. It removes the `x` flag (verbose mode) and refines the JWT token matching pattern to prevent catastrophic backtracking, making it more efficient and resilient against ReDoS attacks. Additionally, a `preserve_delimiter` flag is introduced to control how redacted matches are replaced, ensuring that delimiters are retained when appropriate.

## GHSA-3x94-m842-937c · phpoffice/phpspreadsheet

2026-10-27 · PHP · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `XML_EXTERNAL_ENTITY→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an XML parsing issue in PhpSpreadsheet's ODS reader. Specifically, the library did not properly validate the 'number-rows-repeated' and 'number-columns-repeated' attributes in ODS files. An attacker could craft a malicious ODS file with excessively large values for these attributes or use ISO-2022 escape sequences to bypass XML parsing, leading to resource exhaustion or potentially XXE.

### Impact

An attacker could cause a denial of-service by making the application consume excessive memory or CPU, or potentially achieve XML External Entity (XXE) injection to read local files or perform network requests from the server.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/PhpSpreadsheet/Reader/Ods.php
+++ b/src/PhpSpreadsheet/Reader/Ods.php
@@ -180,7 +182,11 @@ public function listWorksheetInfo(string $filename): array
                         $xml->read();
                         if (self::getXmlName($xml) == 'table:table-row' && $xml->nodeType == XMLReader::ELEMENT) {
                             $rowspan = $xml->getAttribute('table:number-rows-repeated');
-                            $rowspan = empty($rowspan) ? 1 : $rowspan;
+                            $rowspan = empty($rowspan) ? 1 : (int) $rowspan;
+                            self::checkRowsRepeated(
+                                $tmpInfo['totalRows'],
+                                $rowspan
+                            );
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces validation checks for 'number-rows-repeated' and 'number-columns-repeated' attributes to ensure they do not exceed reasonable bounds (e.g., AddressRange::MAX_ROW). It also adds explicit type casting to integers and handles DOMText nodes to prevent unexpected parsing behavior, mitigating both DoS and XXE risks.

## GHSA-f5m9-jfrj-jjj7 · opentelemetry-propagator-jaeger

2026-10-27 · Python · **MODERATE** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The Jaeger baggage propagation mechanism did not impose limits on the number or size of baggage entries when extracting them from an inbound carrier. An attacker could send a large number of baggage items or very large items, causing the application to allocate excessive memory.

### Impact

An attacker could cause a denial of service by sending a specially crafted request with an unbounded amount of baggage data, leading to excessive memory consumption and potential application crashes or slowdowns.

### Key diff

```
--- a/propagator/opentelemetry-propagator-jaeger/src/opentelemetry/propagators/jaeger/__init__.py
+++ b/propagator/opentelemetry-propagator-jaeger/src/opentelemetry/propagators/jaeger/__init__.py
@@ -26,6 +27,11 @@ class JaegerPropagator(TextMapPropagator):
     TRACE_ID_KEY = "uber-trace-id"
     BAGGAGE_PREFIX = "uberctx-"
     DEBUG_FLAG = 0x02
+    # The Jaeger format defines no baggage limits, so the W3C Baggage spec
+    # limits are borrowed to bound an unbounded inbound carrier on extract.
+    MAX_BAGGAGE_ENTRIES = 180
+    MAX_BAGGAGE_ENTRY_BYTES = 4096
+    MAX_BAGGAGE_TOTAL_BYTES = 8192
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces limits on the number of baggage entries (MAX_BAGGAGE_ENTRIES), the size of each entry (MAX_BAGGAGE_ENTRY_BYTES), and the total size of all baggage entries (MAX_BAGGAGE_TOTAL_BYTES). It also refactors the baggage injection and extraction logic to respect these new limits.

## GHSA-fpmh-frq8-7rj8 · github.com/klauspost/compress/zstd

2026-10-27 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from insufficient validation of input data during the decompression process. Specifically, the code did not properly check for excessively large 'tableLog' values in Huffman tables or 'count' values in FSE tables, allowing malformed input to cause panics or lead to an unwritten decoding table.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious compressed input that, when processed by the library, would trigger a panic in the application, leading to a denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/huff0/decompress.go
+++ b/huff0/decompress.go
@@ -54,6 +54,10 @@ func ReadTable(in []byte, s *Scratch) (s2 *Scratch, remain []byte, err error) {
 		if len(in) < int(iSize) {
 			return s, nil, fmt.Errorf("input too small for table, want %d bytes, have %d", iSize, len(in))
 		}
+		// The low nibble is the weights' FSE tableLog - 5, which the format limits to 6.
+		if in[0]&15+5 > 6 {
+			return s, nil, errors.New("corrupt input: weights tableLog too big")
+		}
 		// FSE compressed weights
 		s.fse.DecompressLimit = 255
 		hw := s.huffWeight[:]
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds several validation checks. It now verifies that the 'tableLog' extracted from Huffman table headers does not exceed a maximum allowed value and that 'count' values in FSE tables do not exceed 'math.MaxInt16'. These checks prevent the processing of malformed data that could lead to panics or corrupted internal states.

## GHSA-j2w7-387v-m2rq · phpoffice/phpspreadsheet

2026-10-27 · PHP · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The ODS reader in PhpSpreadsheet did not properly validate the 'table:number-rows-repeated' and 'table:number-columns-repeated' attributes. An attacker could craft an ODS file with extremely large values for these attributes, leading to excessive memory allocation and CPU usage.

### Impact

An attacker could cause a denial of service by providing a malicious ODS file, leading to the application consuming all available memory and CPU resources, making it unresponsive or crashing it.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/PhpSpreadsheet/Reader/Ods.php
+++ b/src/PhpSpreadsheet/Reader/Ods.php
@@ -180,7 +182,11 @@ public function listWorksheetInfo(string $filename): array
                         $xml->read();
                         if (self::getXmlName($xml) == 'table:table-row' && $xml->nodeType == XMLReader::ELEMENT) {
                             $rowspan = $xml->getAttribute('table:number-rows-repeated');
-                            $rowspan = empty($rowspan) ? 1 : $rowspan;
+                            $rowspan = empty($rowspan) ? 1 : (int) $rowspan;
+                            self::checkRowsRepeated(
+                                $tmpInfo['totalRows'],
+                                $rowspan
+                            );
                             $tmpInfo['totalRows'] += $rowspan;
                             $tmpInfo['totalColumns'] = max($tmpInfo['totalColumns'], $currCells);
                             $currCells = 0;
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces validation checks for 'table:number-rows-repeated' and 'table:number-columns-repeated' attributes. It ensures that the repeated counts are positive and do not exceed the maximum allowed row or column limits (AddressRange::MAX_ROW/MAX_COLUMN), throwing an exception if invalid values are encountered.

## GHSA-rxj4-h86g-25gc · phpoffice/phpspreadsheet

2026-10-27 · PHP · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `XML_EXTERNAL_ENTITY→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The `unentity` function in `PhpSpreadsheet\Reader\Xml` did not correctly handle double-encoded XML entities, specifically when `html_entity_decode` was called. This allowed an attacker to bypass the XML scanner's security checks, leading to a Time-of-Check to Time-of-Use (TOCTOU) vulnerability. Additionally, the XML scanner did not properly validate encoding declarations in the presence of a UTF-8 BOM, which could lead to denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious XML file to trigger XML External Entity (XXE) attacks, allowing them to read arbitrary files on the server. They could also cause a denial of service by exploiting the encoding validation bypass.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/PhpSpreadsheet/Reader/Xml.php
+++ b/src/PhpSpreadsheet/Reader/Xml.php
@@ -53,9 +54,14 @@ public function __construct()
 
     public static function unentity(string $contents): string
     {
-        $contents = preg_replace('/&(amp|lt|gt|quot|apos);/', "\u{fffe}\u{feff}$1;", trim($contents)) ?? $contents;
+        // fffe is invalid, replace with replacement char
+        $contents = str_replace("\u{fffe}", "\u{fffd}", $contents);
+        // use positive lookahead to "protect" valid xml entities
+        $contents = Preg::replace('/&(?=(?:amp|lt|gt|quot|apos|#[0-9]+|#x[0-9a-fA-F]+);)/', "\u{fffe}", trim($contents));
+        // now decode remaining html entities
         $contents = html_entity_decode($contents, ENT_NOQUOTES | ENT_SUBSTITUTE | ENT_HTML401, 'UTF-8');
-        $contents = str_replace("\u{fffe}\u{feff}", '&', $contents);
+        // Escape remaining ampersands, restore those which were replaced with fffe
+        $contents = str_replace(['&', "\u{fffe}"], ['&amp;', '&'], $contents);
 
         return $contents;
     }
```

### Fix summary

The `unentity` function was refactored to first replace invalid characters, then 'protect' valid XML entities using a positive lookahead regex before decoding, and finally restore the protected entities. This prevents double-encoding issues. The XML scanner was also updated to check if the encoding declared in the XML matches 'UTF-8' when a UTF-8 BOM is present, preventing encoding-related TOCTOU issues.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 10 occurrences**
> Languages: Python, C#, Java, Ruby, PHP
> → GHSA-3crg-w4f6-42mx
> → GHSA-fwvm-ggf6-2p4x
> → GHSA-7v6w-c3f4-9wpq
> → GHSA-36mm-w85j-3q2j
> → GHSA-cg9x-g3gm-h5h6
> → GHSA-xr9x-r78c-5hrm
> → GHSA-cjx3-73hr-rpw7
> → GHSA-xm28-xvqc-gxxg
> → GHSA-3x94-m842-937c

## GHSA-vg84-c5mg-76xh · github.com/ctfer-io/terraform-provider-ctfd/v2

2026-10-27 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `INFO_DISCLOSURE→ERROR_MESSAGE`

### Root cause

The Terraform provider for CTFd was not marking the 'password' attribute of the team resource as sensitive. This caused the password to be stored in plaintext in the Terraform state file and potentially exposed in logs or UI.

### Impact

An attacker with access to the Terraform state file or logs could read team passwords, leading to unauthorized access to CTFd team accounts.

### Key diff

```
--- a/provider/team_resource.go
+++ b/provider/team_resource.go
@@ -73,6 +73,7 @@ func (r *teamResource) Schema(ctx context.Context, req resource.SchemaRequest, r
 			"password": schema.StringAttribute{
 				MarkdownDescription: "Password of the team. Notice that during a CTF you may not want to update those to avoid defaulting team accesses.",
 				Required:            true,
+				Sensitive:           true,
 				PlanModifiers: []planmodifier.String{
 					stringplanmodifier.RequiresReplace(),
 				},
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds `Sensitive: true` to the 'password' schema attribute for the team resource. This instructs Terraform to treat the password as sensitive data, preventing its exposure in plaintext in state files, logs, and UI outputs.

## GHSA-x94g-w73q-hpcw · dd-trace

2026-10-27 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The application used a regular expression for URL query string obfuscation that was susceptible to catastrophic backtracking. When processing specially crafted input, the regex engine could consume excessive CPU resources, leading to a denial of service.

### Impact

An attacker could send a malicious URL query string to the application, causing the server to become unresponsive or significantly slow down, thereby denying service to legitimate users.

### Key diff

```
-  qs = qs.replace(queryStringObfuscation, '<redacted>')
+  const replacement = queryStringObfuscation === defaultQsObfuscator ? '$1<redacted>' : '<redacted>'
+  qs = qs.replace(queryStringObfuscation, replacement)
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the regex replacement logic to use a more efficient and safer approach. Instead of a simple replacement, it now explicitly captures and preserves the delimiter before a JWT token, preventing the regex from being overly complex and vulnerable to ReDOS.
